Document 172: 9月大涨36%！Meta创2013年来最佳月度表现，市值直逼2万亿美元

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月大涨36%！Meta创2013年来最佳月度表现，市值直逼2万亿美元.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-09-25
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

9 ⽉⾄今， Meta 股价⼤幅反弹， Muse 的快速⾛红重新点燃市场对其 AI 商业化的预期。
Muse 上线 6 天下载量达 90.2 万次，并加速切⼊购物、旅⾏等场景，市场开始押注 AI 代理有
望打开⼴告之外的增⻓空间。但安全隐私⻛险和⾼昂算⼒成本，仍是后续兑现预期的关
键。
摘要：
Meta 正迎来⼀轮由 AI 预期驱动的强势反弹，个⼈ AI 助⼿ Muse 的⾛红成为这⼀轮⾏情的重要催化
剂。
今年以来， Meta 股价⼀度明显承压，但进⼊ 9 ⽉后快速反弹。截⾄ 9 ⽉ 24 ⽇，股价本⽉累计上涨
约 36% ，创 2013 年以来最强单⽉表现，市值重新逼近 2 万亿美元，距离这⼀关⼝仅约 1% 。
⾏情反转的背后， Muse 带来的 AI 商业化想象成为重要⽀撑。 Muse 于 9 ⽉ 8 ⽇正式上线，能够⾃
主执⾏购物、出⾏、邮件管理等多步骤任务。上线前六天下载量达到 90.2 万次，超过 Meta AI 同
期的 77.3 万次，并迅速登顶美国及加拿⼤苹果应⽤商店免费榜。

但资本市场的乐观预期也在迅速升温。 AI 代理开始进⼊购物、预订等真实消费场景，传统互联⽹
平台赖以维持⽤⼾的搜索、账号和使⽤习惯可能受到冲击；与此同时， Muse ⾃⾝的安全漏洞、
隐私争议和⾼昂算⼒成本，也让这场 AI 热潮⾯临现实约束。
从年内弱势到强劲反弹， Meta 迎来 AI 催化
Meta 此前并⾮市场热⻔的 AI 交易。今年 8 ⽉ 18 ⽇前，公司股价⼀度较年初下跌 18% ，位列标普
500 指数年内跌幅最深的 50 只个股之⼀。 7 ⽉底发布的营收指引低于市场预期，也⼀度压制投资
者情绪。
随后，两个因素推动⾏情发⽣变化。⼀⽅⾯， Meta 同意⽀付最⾼ 180 亿美元，就社交媒体相关
诉讼达成和解，⻓期存在的法律不确定性有所缓解；另⼀⽅⾯， Muse 上线后迅速获得⽤⼾关
注，为市场提供了新的 AI 增⻓叙事。
⾃ 8 ⽉低点以来， Meta 股价已反弹约 43% 。摩根⼤通分析师 Doug Anmuth 在 9 ⽉ 10 ⽇将 Meta 评级
从中性上调⾄增持，认为公司正处于推出前沿模型及⼴告之外 AI 产品的早期阶段，仍存在较⼤增

⻓空间。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

这意味着，市场关注点正在从 Meta 传统⼴告业务的增⻓，进⼀步转向 AI 能否贡献新的⽤⼾、收
⼊和估值空间。
Muse 从 “ 聊天 ” ⾛向 “ 执⾏ ” ， AI 代理开始进⼊消费场景
与传统聊天机器⼈相⽐， Muse 更强调直接替⽤⼾完成任务。⽤⼾可以让其执⾏购物下单、旅⾏
预订、邮件起草等多步骤⼯作流，⽬前提供免费层级，付费档位分别为每⽉ 20 美元和 100 美元。
上线后， Meta 持续扩充 Muse 的能⼒边界，包括测试向美国企业发起电话呼叫、开放开发者接⼊
连接器、推出 Mac 版 Muse ，并接⼊ Shop Pay 实现代理结账。 Meta Connect 期间，公司⼜推出
⽤于操作 Muse 的掌上设备 “Muse Charm” ，以及不带摄像头的智能眼镜新品。
商业⽣态也在同步铺开。 Meta 已与 Instacart ⺟公司 Maplebear 达成杂货销售合作，并与 Expedia
建⽴合作关系， Muse 开始从单纯的 AI 助⼿向连接消费服务的平台延伸。

摩根⼤通预计，Muse 有望成为 ChatGPT 之后使⽤最⼴泛的消费级 AI 应⽤之⼀，⽬前⽇下载量已
超过 Meta 旗下其他应⽤总和。不过，该⾏预计 Meta 短期仍将优先推动⽤⼾增⻓和使⽤深度，正
式商业化可能不早于 2027 年。
AI 代理冲击 “ 消费者惯性 ” ，传统平台开始感受压⼒
Muse 的⾛红也开始引发市场对传统消费互联⽹模式的重新审视。⾼盛近期交易台指出，代理式
AI 对 “ 消费者惯性 ” 类股的潜在冲击成为市场热议主题，其追踪的⾼转换成本企业篮⼦指数单⽇下
跌 2.58% 。
市场担忧的核⼼在于，⼀旦 AI 代理能够直接替⽤⼾完成搜索、购物、预订等任务，⽤⼾过去围绕
特定平台形成的使⽤习惯和账号黏性可能被削弱。
Meta 也在主动扩⼤这⼀影响范围。通过接⼊ Maplebear 、 Expedia 等服务， Muse 正在进⼊更具
体的消费链条。对于传统互联⽹平台⽽⾔，未来⽤⼾究竟是直接打开 App ，还是让 AI 代理替⾃⼰
完成操作，可能成为新的竞争变量。

但 Muse ⾃⾝的安全和隐私问题也在快速暴露。
研究⼈员披露， Muse 的 macOS 版本存在零⽇漏洞，本地应⽤或终端命令可能获取⽤于验证⽤⼾
账⼾的令牌，并进⼀步控制⽤⼾账⼾。隐私⽅⾯，也有⽤⼾称 Muse 未经明确授权读取 Mac 通知
内容并据此推送相关建议。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
150 收藏 分享到 :
此外，代码分析发现， Muse 的⾃动化浏览器具备规避机器⼈检测的机制，会修改部分浏览器特
征以降低被识别的概率。亚⻢逊随后封锁 Muse 访问其零售⽹站，相关⾃动化⾏为也成为外界关
注的焦点。
AI 代理的另⼀道⻔槛：算⼒成本
除了安全问题， Muse 的商业模式还⾯临基础设施成本的考验。
按照产品设计，每位⽤⼾理论上拥有⼀台专属云端虚拟机，配置约 2 个虚拟 CPU 、 8GB 内存和
100GB 固态硬盘，并需要全天候运⾏。
粗略测算显⽰，如果 Muse 达到 1 亿⽤⼾， Meta 可能需要部署约 160 万颗 AMD EPYC 处理器、
100PB 内存和 10000PB 固态存储，对应电⼒需求约 1.6 吉⽡。 Meta 今年整体电⼒规划约为 7 吉
⽡，还需要同时满⾜模型训练和⼴告业务。

收⼊端暂时难以覆盖如此庞⼤的投⼊。假设 1000 万⽤⼾订阅每⽉ 20 美元的付费档位，年化收⼊
约 24 亿美元；⽽ Meta 今年资本开⽀预算⾼达 1300 亿⾄ 1450 亿美元。若维持专属虚拟机模式，
Muse 早期的基础设施投⼊仍将⼗分可观。
这也是市场当前需要进⼀步验证的地⽅：AI 代理能够带来多少⽤⼾并不等于能够创造多少利
润。
估值不算激进，但 AI 投⼊正在考验现⾦流
Meta 当前估值仍相对温和。按彭博汇总的分析师⼀致预期，公司未来 12 个⽉预期市盈率约 21
倍，略低于纳斯达克 100 指数的 22 倍。
分析师预计， Meta 2026 年营收增⻓ 26% ⾄ 2540 亿美元，净利润增⻓ 33% ⾄ 806 亿美元；但到
2027 年，营收和利润增速预计分别放缓⾄ 20% 和 9% 。
与此同时， AI 基础设施投⼊正在明显挤压现⾦流。 Meta 去年⾃由现⾦流为 460 亿美元，但受资本
开⽀⼤幅增⻓影响， 2026 年预计转为 -64 亿美元， 2027 年进⼀步降⾄ -292 亿美元。

⽬前，彭博追踪的分析师中超过 90% 对 Meta 维持买⼊评级，但股价已接近分析师平均⽬标价。
经过这⼀轮快速上涨后，市场对 Meta 的 AI 预期已经明显升温， Muse 能否从⽤⼾增⻓进⼀步⾛向
稳定变现，将成为下⼀阶段估值能否继续扩张的关键。

Document 178: [373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속LG에너지솔루션(373220) 원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속LG에너지솔루션(373220) 원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속 - 기업.pdf

### Page 1

2026.09.21   
LG에너지솔루션 
(373220)

원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속 Company Brief

3Q26 원/달러 환율 하락에도 불구하고 영업이익 시장 기대치 상회 전망 
동사의 3Q26 실적은 매출 8.8조원(+45% YoY, + 16% QoQ), 영업이익 3,690억원(-
39% YoY, +226% QoQ)을 기록하며 원/달러 환율 하락으로 매출은 시장 기대치를 
소폭 하회하겠으나 영업이익은 상회할 것으로 전망된다. AMPC 는 미국 ESS 생산량 
증가와 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동에 힘입어 전분기 대비 약 57% 증가한 3,790억원으로 
추정된다. 미국 상호관세 위법 판결에 따른 관세 환급금이나 완성차 업체들의 최소 
구매 물량 미달 보상금 반영 여부에 따라 추가적인 이익 개선 가능성도 존재한다. ESS 
부문은 전분기에서 이연된 물량의 출하가 본격화되면서 3Q26 매출이 전분기 대비 약 
38% 증가할 것으로 예상된다. 특히 미국 ESS 생산라인의 가동 안정화와 AMPC 수혜 
확대에 힘입어 수익성도 점진적으로 개선될 것으로 보인다. 자동차전지 부문도 
3Q26부터 회복세가 강화될 것으로 예상된다. 유럽향 물량 회복과 GM 얼티엄셀즈 
재가동으로 매출이 전분기 대비 큰 폭으로 증가하고, 고정비 부담 완화로 적자폭도 
뚜렷하게 축소될 전망이다. 소형전지 부문은 주요 고객사인 Tesla의 판매 호조에 따른 
안정적인 출하 증가로 매출 회복과 수익성 방어가 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 
4Q26 대규모 ESS 신규 수주 기대 
전세계 ESS 시장은 신재생 에너지 발전 확대와 AIDC 투자 본격화에 힘입어 빠르게 
성장하고 있다. 시장조사기관 SNE리서치에 따르면 1H26 전세계 ESS 배터리 
출하량은 461GWh로 전년 동기 대비 약 71% 증가했다. 이 중 북미 출하량은 
76GWh로 전년 동기 대비 약 83% 증가했으며, 분기별로는 1Q26 35GWh(+91% 
YoY), 2Q26 41GWh(+76% YoY) 를 기록했다. 통상 하반기에 출하가 집중되는 
계절성을 감안하면 올해 북미 ESS 배터리 출하량은 전년 대비 약 70% 증가한 
170~180GWh에 이를 것으로 전망된다. 연초 동사가 예상한 올해 북미 ESS 수요 
성장률 10% 초반 수준을 크게 상회할 가능성이 높은 상황이다. 올해 동사의 ESS 
신규 수주 목표는 약 90GWh이며, 현재까지 약 30GWh의 수주를 확정한 것으로 
파악된다. 이에 시장 일각에서는 연간 수주 목표 달성 여부에 대한 우려가 제기되고 
있다. 그러나 당사가 파악한 바에 따르면 대규모 ESS 신규 수주가 4Q26에 집중될 
것으로 예상된다. 특히 북미 ESS 수요의 가파른 증가세와 투자세액공제(48E)에 
연계된 PFE 공급망 규제 시행은 동사의 수주 확보에 우호적으로 작용할 전망이다. 
매수 투자의견과 목표주가 유지 
동사에 대한 매수 투자의견과 목표주가를 유지한다. 향후 국내 배터리 셀 업체들의 
북미 ESS 신규 수주가 지속적으로 확대될 전망이다. 미국 ESS 시장에서는 신재생 
에너지 발전 확대와 AIDC 투자 본격화에 따른 수요 증가, 중국 공급망에 대한 규제 
강화가 맞물리면서 국내 업체들에 우호적인 사업 환경이 조성되고 있다. 동사 역시 
ESS 중심의 생산라인 전환과 가동률 상승이 본격화될 경우 매출 성장과 함께 고정비 
부담 완화, AMPC 수혜 확대에 따른 수익성 개선이 기대된다. 이를 바탕으로 동사의 
영업이익은 2026년 약 0.8조원에서 2028년 약 5.1조원으로 가파르게 증가할 것으로 
전망된다. ESS의 역할이 신재생 에너지 연계용 저장장치를 넘어 AIDC의 안정적인 
전력 공급을 지원하는 핵심 인프라로 확대되고 있다는 점에도 주목할 필요가 있다. 
이에 따라 동사의 밸류에이션 역시 기존 EV 중심의 평가에서 벗어나 전력 인프라 
산업의 성장성을 반영하는 방향으로 재평가되어야 한다고 판단된다. 업종 내에서 실적 
개선 가시성과 정책 수혜가 뚜렷한 동사에 대한 비중 확대 전략이 유효하다.

 당 보고서에 게재된 내용들은 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
본 분석자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위한 참고자료이며, 따라서, 본 자료에 의한 투자자의 투자결과에 대해 어떠한 목적의 증빙자료로도 사용될 수 없으며, 어떠한 경우에도 작성자 및 
당사의 허가 없이 전재, 복사 또는 대여될 수 없습니다. 무단전재 등으로 인한 분쟁발생시 법적 책임이 있음을 주지하시기 바랍니다.

MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where
inflation levels are generally still above
target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be
watching for fiscal sustainability as
governments implement expansionary
budget plans.

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.
Investment Implications
Underlying earnings-per-share (EPS)
growth of around 30%, stripped
of investment gains, is historically
exceptional. It remains that robust

corporate earnings and positive
revisions have helped Equities absorb
concerns around the recent rise
in yields.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Investing involves risk, including the possible loss of principal. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.
Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and
investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total

MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.

The Front The Problem Bessent's Objective The Weapon

The Yield Rising 10-year Treasury yield Lower/contained yields Debt-management strategy, buybacks, issuance policy

For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

2 BloombergNEF , AI Buildout Drives 76% Power Bill Jump on Largest US Grid, May 14, 2026.

Investment Implications
Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: frayed nerves unsettle expectations
By Elliot Clarke & Ryan Wells
09:53 September 11 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
After reviewing last week’s National Accounts, we revised our RBA call, reinstating a 25bp November
rate hike. Domestic demand is proving more resilient than we had anticipated, buoyed by stronger
household incomes and a large pipeline of data centre and renewable energy investment, limiting the

pace of disinflation.

### Page 2

Markets are pricing in a circa 80% chance of a September rate hike, but we believe the Board will
prefer to wait for confirmation from the full quarterly inflation data and its revised forecasts. Our own
forecast updates will be published in our September Market Outlook later today on Westpac IQ.
This week’s sentiment surveys also served as a reminder that downside risks to growth are still present
in Australia.
Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment buckled in September, the 5.2% decline to 84.4 unwinding much of

August’s gain and leaving sentiment near the pessimistic lows of early-2026.
Cost-of-living pressures clearly remain front of mind, highlighted by the 9.2% drop in ‘family finances vs
a year ago’. The recent rebound in fuel prices, renewed concerns about inflation's persistence and
anxiety over the need for additional monetary tightening all weighed heavily in the month. These
concerns, alongside the downbeat assessment on housing markets, also produced a more pessimistic

reading on broader economic conditions, with both the one-year and five-year ahead measures down
4.0% and 4.5% in the month.
Businesses are hardly brimming with optimism either, the August NAB business survey reporting that
business confidence slipped further into negative territory to –8 in August. Conditions also fell 5pts to –
1, the weakest reading since the pandemic.
Weaker profitability was the chief culprit behind the decline, slumping to its lowest level in years as

firms grapple with softer demand, higher interest rates and elevated input costs. Indeed, gauges of
purchase costs and labour cost growth continue to run at above-average levels, and trading conditions
have weakened to cycle lows. As for the consumer, downside risks persist for business.
Offshore, the flow of data was light ahead of last night’s ECB meeting and tonight’s US CPI update.
Data for the Euro Area was constructive early in the week, GDP growth revised up from 0.4% to 0.6%

in the third estimate for Q2, taking the annual rate up to 1.2%yr. In the quarter, household consumption
showed strength, up 0.4%, and government expenditure lifted 0.2%. Business investment was a partial
offset, edging down 0.1%. Employment also continued to edge up in Q2, maintaining full employment
and indicating marginal labour supply is available to meet demand.
The US PPI subsequently met expectations in August, growth accelerating from a revised 0.1% (prev

0.0%) in July to 0.4%. Excluding food and energy, growth was a touch weaker than expected at 0.2%,
offset by a 0.1ppt upward revision to July to 0.3%. Annual PPI inflation accelerated to 5.4%, or 4.6%
excluding food and energy.
Coming after last Friday’s above expectations August nonfarm payroll release, in which 162k new
positions were reported along with 55k in back revisions to June and July, and given the latest surge in
the price of oil, the August PPI result further solidified the market’s concerns over inflation risks, the US

10-year ranging up to just below 5.0% as a result. At the short end, participants are now debating
whether to price in a fourth hike by the FOMC over the coming year, with three 25bp increases fully
priced by April.

### Page 3

Related articles
As expected, and unsurprisingly given the above data and market developments, the ECB then
delivered a 25bp hike at their September meeting. The risks were characterised as skewed towards
prices, with inflation "to remain well above target for an extended period" primarily as a result of the
effect on energy prices of the Middle East conflict and the Russia / Ukraine war.
The "new ECB staff projections sees headline inflation averaging 3.0% in 2026, 2.5% in 2027 and 2.1%

in 2028". "For inflation excluding energy and food, the baseline foresees 2.5% in 2026, 2.6% in 2027
and 2.3% in 2028." These forecasts are unchanged for 2026, but higher in 2027 and 2028 than in
June. Despite the persistence in inflation and need for tighter policy, the ECB remains constructive on
the growth outlook. "The baseline projection for economic growth is 0.9% for 2026, 1.4% for 2027 and
1.5% for 2028. This is an upward revision for both 2026 and 2027, mainly reflecting the greater than

expected resilience of the euro area economy."
The Governing Council is acutely aware of the uncertainty and risks ahead, and are committed to
determining policy meeting by meeting. From their guidance, it is clear the Council are comfortable with
their current position and capacity to adjust policy in real time to meet their medium-term mandate.

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article  |  25 September 2026   FX
FX Daily: Bond sell-off helps USD
consolidate gains
The rise in back-end yields has prompted a risk-off environment that
favours a consolidation of recent dollar gains. Markets remain reluctant
to price in any optimism into oil prices, which keeps risks on the upside
for rates. EUR/USD may still have some room to fall before recovering
The dollar is benefiting from the knock-on effects of the bond sell-off on global risk sentiment

USD: Keeping an eye on the back-end
The global bond sell-off continues to leave its mark on FX. The dollar is benefiting from the
knock-on effect on global risk sentiment, allowing a consolidation of recent gains even as they
start to look stretched relative to short-term fundamentals. Oil prices remain bid after the UN
summit failed to generate tangible optimism about a resolution in the Gulf.
Reports that the US and Iran are discussing a phased deal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz

prompted a correction in oil, but losses were fully recouped within a couple of hours,
underlining growing scepticism about any imminent de-escalation. It is a dynamic that argues
against any material rebound in bonds for now.
By extension, we remain cautious about calling the end of this USD rally. We may see $110/bbl
for Brent before the end of the month, with the dollar finding fresh support from the energy
story, especially in an environment where the Fed's hawkish remarks are allowing markets to

price in more rate hikes. A testament to that is the 2-year SOFR, which is up almost 20bp over

### Page 2

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 25 September 2026 2
the past 48 hours. Pricing for October has now reached 18bp, with two hikes fully priced in by
January and almost four by July 2027.
Francesco Pesole
EUR: France a rising concern
We still view the 1.1320-1.1330 area as the next key support for EUR/USD. We could reach that
level quite quickly if oil prices take another sharp leg higher and/or US data surprises to the
upside over the next couple of weeks. For now, we do not think new lows are imminent, but

downside risks persist.
As for euro-specific drivers, economic resilience (yesterday’s Ifo index mirrored strong PMIs) is
at least partly being offset as a EUR-positive factor by wider eurozone spreads. French 10-year
yields are trading 110bp above bunds, and there has been little relief from reports that Marine
Le Pen may back the proposed budget to avert a bond crisis.
We analysed France’s fiscal issues in this note. Risks are tilted towards further spread widening

from here, and while the FX impact remains difficult to isolate, it is a narrative that is unlikely
to help the euro in the current environment.
Francesco Pesole
CHF: SNB leaves hawks disappointed
The Swiss National Bank held rates unchanged as expected but surprised markets on the
dovish side yesterday. Despite the upward revision to its inflation projections, policymakers
signalled no concerns about second-round effects, with the price shock still viewed as

temporary.
The SNB slightly tweaked its currency stance, removing the reference to an "increased
willingness" to intervene while retaining its commitment to act when necessary. While this
grabbed headlines, it is merely an adjustment reflecting the franc’s recent weakness and does
not imply any reluctance to intervene again should the currency appreciate.
Market pricing remains too hawkish in our view, and that is what matters most for the Swiss
franc. A hike is already fully priced in by March, but we still see few reasons to tighten policy in

the foreseeable future. We think downside risks remain for the franc, with EUR/CHF potentially
retesting the 0.9480 highs seen earlier in September. USD/CHF is probably where the upside
potential is even greater at this point: an October hike from the Federal Reserve could prompt
a rally to 0.85 in the near term.
Francesco Pesole

### Page 3

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 25 September 2026 3
CEE: Energy shock pushes tightening bets to new highs
Regional markets remain under pressure as volatility in core rates and energy prices amplifies
moves in CEE rates. At the peak, markets priced in an average of 15bp of additional tightening
across the region. Despite a partial correction, rates remain elevated, leaving roughly 125bp of
tightening priced in for both Poland and the Czech Republic. High oil and gas prices at

yesterday’s close, alongside fresh highs in 10-year US Treasury yields, point to continued
stress today. Although the backdrop is not directly comparable with the 2022 energy shock,
the market response is beginning to look similar. With peak tightening expectations in 2022
reaching 210bp in Poland and 165bp in the Czech Republic, further repricing cannot be ruled
out if energy prices continue to rise, regardless of central bank rhetoric.
Yesterday, regional FX found support from improving rate differentials, reversing the narrowing

Document 312: Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23)

23 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
NEXT GENERATION EU: THE PROGRAMME IS COMING TO AN END, BUT ITS EFFECTS ON ECONOMIC ACTIVITY WILL
ENDURE
Guillaume Derrien
A pillar of the NextGenerationEU programme, the Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) will expire on December 31, 2026. As of the
end of August 2026, slightly more than three-quarters of the total budget had been disbursed (EUR 440 billion out of EUR 573 billion).
Disbursing the remaining quarter (EUR 133 billion) by the end of the year will require a significant increase compared with the histo-

rical pace (EUR 90 billion per year). The goal of channeling these funds toward productive investment appears to have been achieved.
Tangible results for investment in Europe
According to Eurostat, public spending co-financed by the RRF totaled EUR 310 billion between 2020 and 2025. Nearly three-quarters (74%)
consisted of capital expenditures, including EUR 106 billion in direct public investment and EUR 122 billion in capital transfers to the private

sector (such as grants). This spending supported investment by businesses and households.
The list of major programmes funded by the RFF, published by the European Commission 1, shows that, within this scope, nearly two-thirds of
the funding went to transportation (primarily rail), energy infrastructure, and digital infrastructure (see Chart 1).
Another notable point: capital expenditures co-financed by the RRF increased significantly, reaching 0.4% of GDP for the European Union as a

whole in 2025 (see Chart 2). There are significant disparities among Member States, primarily due to differences in the amounts allocated to
them. Croatia (1.9%), Greece (1.2%), Italy (1%), and Spain (0.7%) received significant support, in contrast to France and Germany (0.1%). These
figures are consistent with the IMF’s estimate that the RRF added approximately 0.3 percentage points (pp) to EU growth in 2025 (more than
1 pp in Greece and Croatia, around 0.8 pp in Italy, and 0.5 pp in Spain) 2. Furthermore, as the first major joint European borrowing initiative, the

RRF made it possible to finance investment without increasing national budget deficits, giving several countries (Spain, Italy, Greece, Portugal)
valuable breathing room to consolidate their public finances.
1 Recovery and Resilience Scoreboard
2 The EU’s Recovery and Resilience Facility: Experience and Lessons for the Next Multiannual Financial Framework, WP/26/114, June 2026
SOURCE: EUROPEAN COMMISSION, Recovery and Resilience Scorecard
Infrastructure spending accounts for the majority of the funding

Chart of the Week / 23 September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
What impact on EU’s economic activity can we expect once this programme ends?
At first glance, effects will be limited. The end of the programme is expected to weigh on both public and private investment. However, the
strengthening of economic activity that we anticipate (see details in our latest Ecoperspectives – Q3 2026) rests primarily on German public

investment programmes and structural drivers (investment in AI, defence spending).
The ECB makes the same point in its September macroeconomic projections 3. It emphasises that the end of the RRF is expected to weigh
on public investment in 2027, while it anticipates an acceleration in business investment. Furthermore, RRF funds are being absorbed on
the ground only gradually. Since most disbursements are concentrated toward the end of the programme, the effects of the RRF will extend

beyond 2026.
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

Document 338: Switching 9 to 5s Job switching has picked up, with Gen Z leading gains and gender gaps narrowing. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Switching 9 to 5s Job switching has picked up, with Gen Z leading gains and gender gaps narrowing. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-09-01
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

13016291     1

Economy 
Switching 9 to 5s 
09 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job-switching pay premiums have risen to their highest level in more than three years, indicating employers may be willing to 
pay for workers with specific skills, according to Bank of America deposit account data. Gen Z continue to benefit the most from 
changing jobs, while workers paid by the hour are also making particularly large gains from switching. 
• Women's lead in wage gains from job changes has narrowed as men's switching premiums have accelerated faster, returning the 
gap to roughly 2018 levels. Meanwhile, pay disruption rates have fallen and converged across genders, although men's higher 
overall pay levels mean broader earnings gaps remain. 
• AI adoption is not yet translating into broad-based labor displacement, according to BofA Global Research. Job switching 
remains resilient, and industries with the highest AI usage do not yet show consistently weaker labor demand. If that changes, 
younger workers and women look most exposed.

Pay increase associated with a job change hits highest rate since 2023 
The broader US labor market momentum picked up in August (read more on this in the August Institute Employment Report), and 
Bank of America data suggests worker mobility may be showing early signs of improvement. In July, increases in pay associated 
with a job change reached their highest rate in over three years, according to Bank of America account data (Exhibit 1). 
Importantly, the annual average for these pay premiums has inched upwards after consistent yearly declines. 
While job-switching pay premiums have improved in recent months, they remain below pre-pandemic levels. This suggests 
today’s labor market differs from both the Great Resignation period in 2022 and the tighter labor markets of the late 2010s.  
Exhibit 1: In July, the pay increase associated with a job change reached the highest rate in over three years 
After-tax pay change associated with a job change (%, three-month moving average, monthly, annual average)

Source: Bank of America internal data

### Page 2

2 09 September 2026

I NSTI TUTE

Jobs with weekly paychecks see accelerated pay bumps for job switchers 
The premium for switching jobs is most notable among workers with weekly paychecks, though this could also be reflective of 
increased overtime (Exhibit 2). Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) employer payroll data indicates that weekly pay schedules are 
disproportionately concentrated in industries with large hourly workforces and variable hours, particularly construction, leisure 
and hospitality, transportation and warehousing, and portions of retail trade. These jobs often skew lower income. In contrast, 
professional, financial, and other predominantly salaried industries are more likely to use biweekly or semi-monthly payroll 
schedules.  
This acceleration among workers paid weekly provides further evidence that One Big Beautiful Bill Act-related changes to 
overtime hours and tax withholdings could be fueling lower-income wage growth and narrowing differences in spending growth 
across income groups (read more on this in August’s Consumer Checkpoint).  
Exhibit 2: For workers with a weekly paycheck who switched jobs, their change in pay was almost 4x that of those who stayed in their job in July 
After-tax pay change for workers with weekly paychecks by type (monthly, three-month moving average, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Gen Z job change rate outpaces other generations 
Looking across generations, Gen Z have continued to see the greatest pay bumps from switching roles, some of which may 
reflect where they are positioned in the wage distribution (Exhibit 3). Younger workers generally begin their careers at lower 
wage levels, creating more opportunities for substantial percentage increases when they switch jobs. As a result, job changes 
can generate larger proportional pay gains than those typically experienced by older workers. 
Encouragingly, the rate at which Gen Z change jobs has grown and accelerated year-over-year (YoY) since February, surpassing 
all other generations for the first time since 2021 (Exhibit 4). This uptick is particularly noteworthy because it comes at a time 
when many broader labor market indicators suggest slower hiring.  
Gen Z's willingness to keep changing jobs may indicate that opportunities remain available for early-career workers even as 
employers become more selective overall. On the other hand, it could also suggest that companies are prioritizing lower-cost 
entry-level hires over replacing more experienced workers. 
Exhibit 3: Gen Z have continued to see the greatest pay change 
rates from switching jobs

### Page 3

09 September 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

Women's job-switching premium is growing more slowly than men's 
Women are still getting a bigger raise than men when they switch jobs, but the difference between women’s and men’s pay 
change from a job change is back to 2018 levels (Exhibit 5). And though women used to change jobs at a faster rate, men’s job 
change rate has edged out women’s since 2024, according to Bank of America account data. 
In our view, the narrowing difference does not necessarily indicate weaker labor market outcomes for women. Instead, it appears 
to reflect faster recent acceleration in men's job-switching wage gains. As a result, the job-switching premium has become more 
similar across genders than it was during much of the post-pandemic period. 
However, the shrinking gap here could pose challenges for pay equality because women’s level of pay often starts from a lower 
base compared to men’s (read more on this in March’s Where are women positioned in a “K-shaped” economy?). Since job 
switching is often one of the fastest ways workers increase earnings, differences in job-switching premiums can have enduring 
effects on wage growth over time. 
Exhibit 5: The difference between women’s and men’s pay change from a job change is back to 2018 levels 
After-tax pay change from a job change by gender (annual average year-to-date, % difference)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Gender gap in pay disruptions narrows to near record low 
Similarly, the gap between women’s and men’s pay disruption rate – which we view as a proxy for temporary or permanent labor 
force departures – has narrowed and both rates have moderated since the start of the year (Exhibit 6) (see Methodology - Pay 
Disruption Rate). The labor market has become more equal on this measure, but not because women's disruption rates have 
fallen dramatically. Rather, men's disruption rates have increasingly resembled women's. 
Interestingly, this is against the backdrop of fewer men in the labor force. Since January, data from the BLS indicates men’s 
growth in the civilian labor force aged 25-54 has fallen, whereas women’s has continued to increase (Exhibit 7).  
Exhibit 6: The pay disruption rate for both men and women has 
fallen over the past few months

### Page 4

I NSTI TUTE

AI anxiety is not yet evident in job change rate 
Job-switching behavior may be an important early indicator because labor market adjustments often show up in mobility before 
employment. If firms become more cautious about hiring in the wake of AI adoption, workers may first become less willing or 
less able to switch jobs before broader effects emerge in payroll or unemployment data.  
So, the fact that we’re seeing an increase in job switching is a sign of a somewhat tight labor market despite overall payrolls 
slowing.  
Information and finance/insurance show early signs of AI labor demand impact 
Using a measure of AI usage from the Census Bureau's Business Trend and Outlook Survey (BTOS), which asks respondents if 
they've used AI in the last two weeks for any business function, BofA Global Research found two industries worth watching: 
information as well as finance and insurance. Both have high AI usage and have seen labor demand decline over the five-month 
period ending in June (Exhibit 8). 
However, the lack of a clear relationship in the overall picture suggests AI adoption is not yet translating into broad-based labor 
displacement. In many industries, businesses may still be experimenting with AI tools or using them to augment workers rather 
than replace them.  
Exhibit 8: At this time, there is not a clear relationship between AI usage levels and growth in labor demand overall 
AI usage levels versus labor demand through June

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Women and younger workers face the greatest AI-related labor market risks 
Although there is no firm AI impact yet, women are more exposed to AI than men, largely because they are overrepresented in 
administrative roles with routine, language-based tasks well suited to generative AI automation or augmentation, according to 
the International Labor Organization (read more on this in May’s Adaptability is the new job security: AI and the future of work). 
BLS forecasts confirm this – demand is expected to be limited for occupations such as procurement clerks; legal secretaries and 
administrative assistants; and customer service representatives. 
And specifically in AI-related roles, women made up just over a quarter of new hires in the US last year — compared with half of 
new hires in non-AI occupations — according to data from a LinkedIn study.1 This suggests that women could be more 
susceptible to the possible negative impact of AI on jobs in more ways than one. 
Younger workers also look potentially exposed because they are more likely to hold roles with automatable tasks and where AI 
adoption is occurring more rapidly (read more on Gen Z’s labor market position in August’s Gen Z reality check). Still, certain 
roles with very high AI exposure2 for recent graduates are expected to have strong growth over the next ten years, according to 
BLS employment occupation projections (Exhibit 9).

1 LinkedIn. (Aug 18, 2026). New LinkedIn Research Finds Women Account for Just 26% of AI Hires as AI Jobs Surge. LinkedIn. 
2 AI exposure indicates potential task transformation – not job loss.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-
peer, cash and checks.  
Median annual income growth is derived from customers who have a valid income value for every month over the time period and who have a 
non-null gender code. Gender data is self-select. 
The Pay Disruption Rate is defined as the proportion of customers who previously had 12 months of regular payroll payments into their 
accounts, followed by at least 3 and up to 6 months of no payments, relative to the total number of customers with 12 consecutive months of 
payroll. 
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago who have a non-null gender code.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

89.5
Above 100: More New Zealanders are optimistic about
the economic environment than are pessimistic.
Below 100: More New Zealanders are pessimistic
about the economic environment than are optimistic.
Confidence springs back up, but there’s still a lot of nervousness

out there.
• Spring is here. The mornings are getting lighter. And in Auckland the CRL is
FINALLY open. Against that backdrop, consumers across the country are feeling
a bit less downbeat about the economic outlook. Even so, there is still a lot of
nervousness about economic conditions.
• Consumer confidence rose 9 points in our latest Westpac McDermott-Miller
survey of New Zealand households, taking it to a level of 89.5. That’s a sizeable
lift after confidence fell to a three-year low earlier in the year. But even with this

recent pickup, consumer confidence is still well below average levels (Note:
a level below 100 means that there are more households who are pessimistic
about economic conditions than those who are optimistic).
• Since our last survey in June, some of the pressure on households’ finances
has eased. Most notably petrol prices have fallen from the eyewatering highs
we saw earlier this year (though at the time of writing, the outlook for petrol
prices is looking worrying again). We’ve also seen ongoing strength in key export

industries, which has been boosting incomes and sentiment in many regions.
• But even with those factors supporting sentiment, households are still being
buffeted by some powerful financial headwinds and they remain cautious about
their spending.

% of households who said their

Dubai oil (USD/barrel)
Not a lot left in the tank.
• The big concern for households remains the pressure on their finances.
Even with a modest improvement since our last survey, the vast majority of

households continue to report that their financial position has deteriorated over
the past year. And households aren’t expecting an improvement over 2027, with
economic conditions expected to remain sluggish.
• The pressure on households finances is coming on several fronts, with fuel costs
still a key concern. Even though petrol prices have fallen since their spike in the
early part of the year, prices at the pump aren’t low, with 91 unleaded currently

averaging around $3.12/ltr around the country. And with global fuel prices
pushing higher again in recent days, the risks for local petrol prices are firmly to
the upside.
• But it’s not just fuel prices that are worrying households. The past year has also
seen large increases in other living costs, along with continued softness in the
labour market.
• Importantly, the strength in inflation has seen the RBNZ hiking the Official Cash
Rate twice since July, and we expect further increases over the months ahead.

That’s seen upward pressure on mortgage interest rates.

Pumping the brakes.
 • With living costs and interest rates pushing higher, households are still very 
cautious about pulling out their credit cards.
 • The majority of households we spoke to told us they’ve cut back their 
spending on dining out and other entertainment activities. Households are 
similarly cautious about purchasing major items for the home, like appliances 
or furnishings.
 • But it’s not just discretionary purchases that households have become more 
cautious abouts since oil prices have spiked. In our separate survey in July, 
around 30% of households we spoke to said they’ve adjusted their spending on 
essentials like groceries, purchasing fewer or cheaper items. Many households 
also told us they’ve been driving less.

Index Index
Wellington and Auckland continuing to lag rural regions.
• While still below average, confidence has taken a step higher in nearly every

part of the country. That’s consistent with lower fuel prices putting money back
into households’ wallets right across the nation.
• The only exception to this pattern was in Taranaki/Manawatu-Whanganui, where
confidence was essentially unchanged this quarter and still very low. Regions in
the central and lower North Island are seeing some mixed economic conditions,
with firmness in sectors like agriculture, but broader softness in the labour
market and house prices.

• Even with confidence picking up across most of the country this quarter, there
are still big differences in economic conditions across regions. Those regions
with strong rural backbones are continuing to outperform the major urban
centres of Auckland and Wellington. In addition, labour market conditions have
been notably firmer in the lower South Island than elsewhere.
